26:T5f4,The word "latte" is borrowed from the Italian caffè latte, meaning "milk coffee." But the drink that most of us picture (espresso topped with steamed milk and a thin layer of foam) is largely an American invention. In Italy, a caffè latte is a simple morning drink made at home, usually with stovetop moka pot coffee poured into a glass of hot milk. No art, no foam, no ceremony. The elaborate coffeehouse version emerged in Seattle in the 1980s, when espresso bars started experimenting with milk textures and presentation.

In the Gulf, the latte arrived through the wave of international coffee chains that opened across Dubai, Abu Dhabi, Riyadh, and Doha in the late 1990s and early 2000s. Coffee culture in the region had always been strong, with gahwa (Arabic coffee) and karak chai deeply embedded in daily life, but the milky, sweetened latte found a natural audience. Gulf coffee drinkers often prefer their drinks rich and sweet, and the latte fit right in. Today, it is one of the most ordered drinks in the region, and local roasters have started adding Gulf touches: cardamom lattes, saffron lattes, date-syrup sweetened versions.

Making a good latte at home comes down to two things: strong, concentrated coffee and properly textured milk. The coffee needs to be bolder than a standard drip brew because it has to hold its flavor against 8 ounces of milk. And the milk needs to be steamed to a smooth, glossy consistency: tiny bubbles, no foam chunks. Get those two right, and you are most of the way there.27:T669,Machboos (also spelled majboos or machbous depending on which part of the Gulf you are in) is the dish that sits at the center of Khaleeji food culture. Every Gulf country claims a version, and families argue over the details the way Italian families argue over Sunday ragù. The name comes from the Arabic word "kabsa" or "machboos" meaning "pressed," referring to the method of cooking rice pressed down with meat so the grains absorb every drop of flavor from the broth.

The dish traces its roots to the ancient trade routes that passed through the Arabian Peninsula. The core technique, cooking rice in a spiced meat broth, arrived from Persia and the Indian subcontinent centuries ago, but Gulf cooks made it their own through one ingredient that defines the entire cuisine: loomi, the sun-dried black lime. These small, hard, dark spheres are made by boiling fresh limes in salt water and drying them in the sun for weeks. The result is a flavor that does not exist anywhere else in cooking: sour, smoky, earthy, and faintly sweet all at once. It is the single ingredient that makes machboos taste like machboos and not like biryani.

In the UAE and Bahrain, machboos is typically made with chicken or lamb and served on a communal platter for Friday family lunches. In Kuwait, the version tends to be slightly wetter and more tomato-heavy. In Saudi Arabia, a closely related dish called kabsa uses a slightly different spice profile. But across the entire Gulf, the principle is the same: rice cooked in richly flavored broth, meat served on top, and the whole thing brought to the table on a single large platter meant to be shared.28:T62f,Halwa puri is Pakistan's answer to the full English breakfast: a multi-dish spread that takes time, fills the house with noise and aroma, and turns a lazy morning into an event. The tradition is strongest in Punjab, particularly in Lahore, where halwa puri shops line every neighborhood and the Sunday morning queue starts before sunrise. But it belongs to all of Pakistan, with regional variations from Karachi to Peshawar, and it has traveled wherever Pakistanis have settled.

The breakfast is ancient in its components. Puri, unleavened deep-fried bread, has roots in the Indian subcontinent going back at least a thousand years. Sooji ka halwa, the sweet semolina dish, is descended from Arabic and Persian confections that arrived with the Mughal Empire. And channay, the spiced chickpea curry, draws on a tradition of legume cooking that has sustained South Asian populations for centuries. Somewhere along the way, these three elements merged into a single meal, and that meal became inseparable from the rhythm of Pakistani weekend life.

In the Gulf, halwa puri has become one of the most recognizable Pakistani food traditions. With roughly three million Pakistanis living across the UAE, Saudi Arabia, Qatar, Kuwait, Oman, and Bahrain, the Friday morning breakfast has been recreated in apartments from Sharjah to Jeddah. The recipes stay close to home (the same ghee-heavy halwa, the same hand-rolled puris, the same arguments about whether the channay should be dark or light), but the setting is different, and that difference has made the ritual even more meaningful.29:T5c6,Balaleet is one of the most distinctive breakfast dishes in the Gulf: a plate that combines sweet, saffron-scented vermicelli noodles with a thin savory omelet folded on top. The contrast surprises first-time tasters, but in the Gulf it makes perfect sense. Sweet and savory combinations run through Khaleeji cooking like a thread, from dates eaten with cheese to saffron rice served with meat stews.

The dish has a history shaped by trade. Vermicelli noodles are not native to the Arabian Peninsula. They arrived through centuries of commercial exchange with India and Southeast Asia. Gulf merchants who traded in spices, pearls, and textiles brought back not only goods but also cooking techniques, and the thin wheat noodles became a pantry staple across the region. Gulf cooks adapted them by toasting the noodles in ghee until golden (which gives balaleet its distinctive nutty depth) and sweetening them with sugar, saffron, cardamom, and rosewater, the same aromatic trio that appears in almost every traditional Gulf dessert.

Balaleet is served across the UAE, Bahrain, Kuwait, and Qatar, though every family insists their version is the correct one. Some add a pinch of cinnamon. Others use orange blossom water instead of rosewater. The omelet on top ranges from a thin French-style crepe to a thicker, more substantial egg layer. It is traditionally a breakfast dish, served during Eid celebrations and Ramadan iftars, but many Gulf families eat it any time of day.2a:T667,Adobo is the dish most often called the national food of the Philippines, though Filipinos themselves will tell you there is no single national dish, just this one that every family cooks and no two families make the same way. The name is Spanish, borrowed during three centuries of colonial rule, but the technique is older than the colonizers. Filipino cooks were preserving meat in vinegar long before the Spanish arrived and gave the method a European name.

The basic principle (braising protein in vinegar and salt) was originally a preservation technique in a tropical climate where meat spoiled quickly. The vinegar's acidity slowed bacterial growth and extended the life of the food. When the Spanish introduced soy sauce to the Philippines through the Manila-Acapulco galleon trade (which also brought ingredients from China and Southeast Asia), Filipino cooks added it to the braise, and modern adobo was born. The soy sauce contributed salt, color, and umami. The vinegar provided tang and preservation. Together, they created something greater than either ingredient alone.

In the Gulf, adobo has become one of the most widely cooked Filipino dishes, prepared nightly in apartments from Abu Dhabi to Dammam by a community of over two million Filipino workers and families. The recipe adapts easily to Gulf kitchens: the ingredients are universally available, it cooks in a single pot, and it reheats beautifully the next day (many Filipinos will tell you it tastes better on day two). It has also crossed cultural lines, winning over Arab, Indian, and Pakistani colleagues through office potlucks and neighborly food sharing.2b:T678,Salona is the everyday stew of the Gulf: the dish that appears on weeknight dinner tables more often than any celebratory rice platter. The word comes from the same root as "salon" in some South Asian languages, meaning a gravy or curry, and the connection is not accidental. Gulf cooking absorbed influences from the Indian subcontinent through centuries of trade and migration, and salona is one of the clearest examples of that exchange: a spiced, broth-based stew that feels familiar to anyone who has eaten South Asian food, but tastes distinctly Gulf.

What makes a salona Gulf rather than Indian is the combination of loomi (dried black lime), baharat, and tamarind. These three ingredients create a flavor profile that is earthy, sour, and aromatic in a way that Indian curries typically are not. The tamarind adds a fruity tang, the loomi contributes a smoky depth, and the baharat blend provides warmth without the sharp heat of Indian chili powder. Gulf salona is also lighter than most Indian curries. The gravy is thinner, more brothy, designed to be spooned over rice rather than mopped up with bread.

Fish salona is the coastal version of this dish, and it is arguably the most traditional. The Gulf's coastal communities (in the UAE, Oman, Bahrain, Kuwait, and eastern Saudi Arabia) have fished these waters for thousands of years, and fish stews were a daily staple long before rice and meat dishes became associated with celebrations. Hamour (grouper) is the most prized fish for salona, though any firm white fish works. The fish is added to the stew at the very end, cooking gently in the simmering broth so it stays intact and tender.2c:T5fd,Karak chai is the Gulf's unofficial national drink. Not gahwa (the pale, cardamom-heavy Arabic coffee served at formal occasions) but this thick, sweet, milky tea that people drink three, four, five times a day. It is sold from every cafeteria window and roadside kiosk in the UAE, Qatar, Bahrain, Kuwait, and Oman. It costs a dirham or two. It is never refused.

The word "karak" comes from the Hindi-Urdu word for "strong," and the drink's origins trace directly to the South Asian tea tradition brought to the Gulf by Indian and Pakistani workers in the mid-twentieth century. As the Gulf states began developing their oil economies in the 1960s and 70s, hundreds of thousands of workers from the subcontinent arrived, bringing their chai with them. The Gulf version evolved into something distinct: stronger, sweeter, made with evaporated milk instead of fresh, and flavored with crushed cardamom and sometimes a pinch of saffron.

What makes karak chai different from a standard masala chai is the technique as much as the ingredients. The tea and milk are boiled together: not just simmered, but brought to a rolling boil repeatedly. Each time the mixture rises in the pot, the cook lowers the heat and lets it settle, then brings it back to a boil again. This process, repeated three or four times, concentrates the flavor and gives karak its signature rich, almost caramelized taste. The evaporated milk (not condensed, which would be too sweet) is essential: it has a density and creaminess that fresh milk cannot replicate.2d:T632,Shakshuka (eggs poached in a spiced tomato sauce) is one of those dishes claimed by half the Mediterranean and Middle Eastern world, and no one is entirely sure who made it first. The name is Arabic, likely derived from a word meaning "mixture" or "shaken." North Africans, particularly Tunisians and Libyans, have strong claims to the dish. So do Yemenis, who make a version called shakshouka that predates the more famous Israeli adoption. The Ottomans probably spread it across their empire. The truth is that eggs cooked in tomato sauce is such an obvious, satisfying idea that it almost certainly arose independently in multiple places.

In the Gulf, shakshuka arrived through multiple channels: from Levantine and Yemeni communities, from Egyptian and North African expats, and eventually from the global food media that turned it into a brunch trend in the 2010s. Gulf cooks adapted it by adding their own spice blends. This version uses bezar (the signature Gulf spice mixture of cumin, coriander, black pepper, cinnamon, and sometimes dried chilies) in place of the cumin-paprika-cayenne combination typical of North African versions. The result is warmer and more aromatic, less smoky, with a spice profile that sits comfortably alongside other Gulf breakfast dishes like balaleet and foul medames.

The beauty of shakshuka is its simplicity. One pan, a handful of ingredients, twenty-five minutes from stove to table. It is the kind of recipe that forgives improvisation (you can add feta, olives, preserved lemon, or whatever is in the fridge) and still delivers every time.2e:T644,Baklava is one of the oldest pastries still made in its original form. Versions of layered nut-filled pastry drenched in honey or syrup appear in records from the Assyrian Empire, and the dish was refined into something close to its modern form in the kitchens of the Ottoman Topkapi Palace in Istanbul. From there, it spread across the entire former Ottoman world (from Greece and Turkey to Lebanon, Syria, Iran, and the Arabian Gulf), and each region developed its own version.

The Gulf's baklava tradition leans heavily on two ingredients: pistachios and cardamom. Turkish baklava often uses walnuts and is drenched in butter-heavy syrup. Greek baklava favors honey and cinnamon. Lebanese versions use a mix of nuts with orange blossom water. Gulf baklava is built around the bright green pistachios imported from Iran (the Gulf's historic trading partner) and perfumed with cardamom and rosewater, the same aromatic combination that defines Gulf sweets from luqaimat to kunafa.

In the Gulf, baklava is associated with generosity and celebration. It appears at Eid gatherings, weddings, and Ramadan iftar tables. It is given as a gift when visiting someone's home and offered with Arabic coffee to guests. The quality of a host's baklava (crisp layers, generous nut filling, well-balanced syrup) is a quiet measure of hospitality, and many Gulf families order from specific bakeries they have patronized for decades. Making it at home is more ambitious than most Gulf desserts, but the result, when you get those shattering-crisp phyllo layers and the syrup soaks in just right, is worth the effort.2f:T5d2,Muhalla (sometimes spelled muhallaq, muhallabiyya, or chebab depending on where in the Gulf you are) is a traditional Emirati and Gulf Arab pancake or crepe that predates the region's modern development by generations. It belongs to the same family of griddle breads found across the Middle East and North Africa, from Moroccan baghrir to Yemeni lahoh, but the Gulf version distinguishes itself with saffron and rosewater, the two aromatics that run through Khaleeji cooking like a signature.

The dish has deep roots in Emirati culture. Older Emiratis remember muhalla as a staple of their grandmothers' kitchens, made on flat iron griddles over charcoal fires. The batter was simple (flour, eggs, a little sugar), but the saffron gave it a golden color and a fragrance that announced itself through the house. It was a breakfast food, a snack, a Ramadan staple. Date syrup (dibs) was the traditional accompaniment, drizzled over the warm crepes while they were still steaming.

Today, muhalla occupies a special place in Gulf food culture as one of the dishes that connects modern Emirati life to its pre-oil past. It appears on heritage restaurant menus and at Ramadan tents, and it is one of the recipes that Emirati food preservation projects have worked to document and keep alive. The version here uses a crepe-style batter rather than the slightly thicker traditional pancake, producing lighter, more delicate results that work beautifully with the date syrup and pistachio garnish.30:T59c,Mutabbaq samak is Kuwait's signature fish and rice dish, and it carries the memory of a time when Kuwait's economy ran on the sea rather than on oil. Before the mid-twentieth century, Kuwait was a maritime nation. Its wooden dhows sailed as far as East Africa and India, and its coast supported a thriving fishing and pearl-diving industry. Fish was not a luxury. It was the daily protein of a coastal people, and mutabbaq samak was one of the ways they ate it.

The name means "covered fish" or "layered fish," referring to the way the fried fish is placed on top of the seasoned rice, covering it like a lid. The dish is closely related to machboos, but where machboos cooks the meat in the rice broth, mutabbaq samak keeps the fish separate, frying it whole until crisp and golden before laying it over the finished rice. This gives the fish a texture that braised or stewed fish cannot achieve: a crunchy exterior giving way to tender, flaky flesh underneath.

Zubaidi (silver pomfret) is the traditional fish for this dish and is considered the finest eating fish in the Gulf. It has a clean, mild flavor and a firm texture that holds up well to frying. When zubaidi is unavailable or out of season, sea bream, hamour (grouper), or any firm-fleshed white fish will work. The rice is cooked with deeply caramelized onions and baharat, giving it a golden color and a sweet, spiced depth that complements the clean flavor of the fish.31:T707,Harees is one of the oldest dishes in the Arabian Peninsula, and its history stretches back far beyond the Gulf states as we know them today. References to harisa (a wheat-and-meat porridge from which harees descends) appear in medieval Arabic cookbooks dating to the tenth century. The Abbasid caliphs ate it. Crusaders encountered it. Versions of it exist from Armenia to Hyderabad, each adapted to local grains and meats, but all sharing the same fundamental idea: wheat and protein, cooked slowly and for a very long time, until they merge into a smooth, comforting porridge.

In the Gulf, harees holds a particular cultural weight because of its association with Ramadan. During the holy month, it is served at nearly every iftar table, the meal that breaks the day's fast at sunset. Its soft, easy-to-digest texture makes it ideal for a stomach that has been empty since dawn, and its richness provides sustenance for the evening ahead. Many Gulf families consider Ramadan incomplete without harees, and in some households, the recipe and technique have been handed down through four or five generations.

The dish requires almost nothing in terms of ingredients (cracked wheat, meat, water, salt, ghee), but it demands patience. Traditional harees is cooked for three to four hours over the lowest possible heat, stirred occasionally, until the wheat grains disintegrate completely and the meat falls apart into fibers that disappear into the porridge. The final texture should be absolutely smooth, almost like a savory cream. It is then served in a bowl with a well of melted ghee in the center and a dusting of cinnamon. The simplicity is the point. In a cuisine built on complex spice blends and layered rice dishes, harees strips everything away and lets two ingredients do all the work.32:T76b,Shorbat adas (lentil soup) is one of those dishes that belongs to everyone and no one. You will find versions of it from Cairo to Karachi, from Istanbul to Aden. But the Gulf version has its own identity, shaped by centuries of trade and the practical realities of cooking in a region where fresh produce was historically scarce and pantry staples had to travel well. Lentils, dried spices, onions, and lemons: everything in this soup could survive a long journey by dhow or caravan.

The dish has roots that go back thousands of years. Lentils are one of the oldest cultivated crops in human history, domesticated in the Fertile Crescent around 8,000 BCE. The biblical story of Esau selling his birthright for a bowl of lentil stew speaks to how central this humble legume was to the ancient Near East. Arab traders carried lentils and the tradition of lentil soups across the peninsula, and by the medieval period, cookbooks from Baghdad and Damascus included dozens of lentil preparations.

In the Gulf today, shorbat adas holds a particular place during Ramadan. After a full day of fasting, the body needs something gentle and nourishing to break the fast, not a heavy meal that shocks the stomach. A bowl of warm lentil soup does exactly that. It is light enough to ease you back into eating, rich enough to begin restoring energy, and quick enough that even a tired cook can have it ready before the maghrib prayer call. Most families serve it alongside dates and water as the first course of iftar, before the main dishes come out.

What separates the Gulf version from its cousins elsewhere is the finishing. A drizzle of good olive oil, a hard squeeze of fresh lemon, and sometimes a sprinkle of sumac or a scattering of fried garlic chips on top. The cumin is toasted until it smells almost smoky. The soup is usually blended smooth, not left chunky. Gulf palates tend to prefer it silky.33:T801,Shawarma is street food royalty across the Middle East, and its origins are tangled up in the broader history of spit-roasted meat that stretches from the Ottoman Empire to modern-day Beirut. The word itself comes from the Turkish "çevirme," meaning "turning," a reference to the vertical rotisserie that defines the dish. Stacks of marinated meat rotate slowly next to a wall of heat, the outer layer crisping and caramelizing while the inside stays juicy. The cook shaves thin slices off the spinning cone as it cooks, and those slices (charred at the edges, tender in the middle) are what make shawarma shawarma.

The technique traveled from the Ottoman territories into the Arab world in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, and every country put its own stamp on it. In Lebanon and Syria, chicken shawarma is marinated in a bright, tangy blend heavy on lemon and garlic. In Egypt, it leans toward cumin and vinegar. In the Gulf states, shawarma arrived with Levantine immigrants who opened small restaurants and street stalls in the rapidly growing cities of the 1970s and 1980s. Today, shawarma joints are as common in Dubai, Riyadh, and Doha as they are in Beirut. You will find them on nearly every commercial street, usually wedged between a laundry and a grocery, identifiable by the slowly spinning cone of meat visible through the window.

What makes Gulf shawarma distinct is the context more than the recipe. It is eaten at all hours: a 2 AM shawarma run after a late night is as much a Gulf tradition as morning karak. The wraps tend to be generous, the garlic sauce (toum) is applied with abandon, and the pickled turnips that come on the side have stained every styrofoam container in the region a deep, unapologetic pink.

Making shawarma at home means you do not have a vertical rotisserie, and that is fine. The flavor comes from the marinade and the char, not the equipment. Chicken thighs marinated in yogurt and spices, then cooked at high heat until the edges blacken slightly, get you remarkably close to the real thing.34:T524,Chicken thighs are the single best cut of meat for the air fryer. They have enough fat to stay juicy at high heat, a skin that crisps beautifully in circulating air, and a forgiving nature that means you can cook them a minute too long without disaster. Breast meat dries out. Wings are fiddly. Thighs just work.

The key to perfect air fryer chicken thighs is dry skin. Moisture is the enemy of crispness, so a thorough pat-down with paper towels before seasoning is non-negotiable. After that, a simple rub of oil, garlic powder, smoked paprika, salt, and pepper is all you need. The air fryer does the rest: the circulating heat renders the fat layer under the skin while drying and crisping the surface, producing results that rival deep frying with a fraction of the oil.

In Gulf kitchens, chicken thighs are a weeknight staple. They are affordable, available everywhere from hypermarkets to corner baqalas, and they pair well with everything from basmati rice and salad to flatbread and hummus. The air fryer has made them even more practical: no preheating a full oven, no splatter from a frying pan, and dinner on the table in 25 minutes.

**Pro Tip:** Always cook chicken thighs skin-side up in the air fryer. The fat renders downward, basting the meat, while the skin faces the heating element and crisps.35:T576,Wings are one of the hardest things to get right without a deep fryer, and one of the easiest things to get right with an air fryer. The circulating heat renders the fat under the skin and crisps it from every angle, producing results that rival restaurant-quality fried wings. The secret weapon is baking powder. A light dusting mixed into the seasoning raises the skin's pH, which breaks down proteins and draws out moisture during cooking. The result is skin that crisps faster and stays crunchier longer.

The technique requires patience in one area: temperature staging. Starting the wings at a lower temperature (180°C) renders the fat slowly and evenly. Finishing at a higher temperature (220°C) blasts the skin to a deep golden crunch. This two-stage approach is what separates great air fryer wings from merely good ones.

In the Gulf, chicken wings have become a fixture at gatherings, from Friday family dinners to football watch parties. Every shawarma shop and casual restaurant has them on the menu, and the home air fryer has made it possible to produce the same quality on a weeknight. The three sauces below (classic buffalo, honey garlic, and dry salt-and-pepper) cover the range from fiery to sweet to simple.

**Pro Tip:** Use a wire rack if your air fryer has one. Elevating the wings allows rendered fat to drip away, keeping the bottoms crispy instead of sitting in grease.36:T5e0,Sweet potatoes and air fryers are a perfect match. The concentrated, circulating heat caramelises the natural sugars in sweet potato far more effectively than a conventional oven, producing deeply golden, almost candy-like edges on fries and a rich, caramel-scented flesh on whole baked ones. The results feel indulgent but the method is remarkably healthy: barely any oil, no deep frying, and sweet potatoes are packed with fiber, vitamin A, and potassium.

This recipe gives you two methods: a whole baked sweet potato for when you want something simple and satisfying, and crispy sweet potato fries for when you want a snack or side dish that disappears the moment it hits the table. The fries require one specific trick to avoid sogginess: cornstarch. A light dusting absorbs surface moisture and creates a thin, crispy shell that stays crunchy for several minutes after cooking. Without it, sweet potato fries go limp almost immediately because of their high moisture content.

In the Gulf, sweet potatoes have become increasingly popular over the past decade, driven partly by health-conscious eating trends and partly by their natural sweetness, which appeals to Gulf palates. You will find them roasted at street food stalls, baked at health-focused cafes, and served as fries at casual restaurants across Dubai, Riyadh, and Doha.

**Pro Tip:** For fries, soak the cut sweet potato in cold water for 30 minutes before cooking. This removes excess starch and moisture, resulting in crispier fries.37:T503,The air fryer makes surprisingly excellent cookies, and the case for baking them this way is not just speed (though 8 minutes versus 12 to 15 in an oven is nice). The real advantage is texture. The circulating hot air crisps the edges of the cookie while the center stays soft and slightly underdone, creating that bakery-style contrast between crunch and chew that home ovens often struggle to achieve.

The other advantage is portion control. This is a small-batch recipe that makes exactly 6 cookies. No scaling up a giant recipe and ending up with three dozen cookies that call to you from the kitchen counter all week. You mix the dough, scoop it, and eight minutes later you have a warm dessert, enough for two people with a couple left over, or enough for one person who has had a day.

The dough itself is a straightforward brown-butter chocolate chip cookie base. Browning the butter takes two extra minutes but adds a nutty, caramel-like depth that separates these from basic cookies. If you are in a rush, melted (not browned) butter works fine. The cookies will be less complex in flavor but still excellent.

**Pro Tip:** Chill the dough balls for 15 minutes in the fridge before air frying. Cold dough spreads more slowly, producing thicker cookies with better texture.38:T62c,Tofu in the air fryer is a revelation for anyone who has struggled with soggy, bland tofu on the stovetop. The circulating hot air crisps every surface of the cube evenly, producing a crunch that rivals deep frying, without a pot of oil or the mess that comes with it. The key is pressing the tofu first to remove excess water, then marinating it briefly so it absorbs flavor before the cornstarch coating goes on.

Most tofu recipes fail for one of two reasons: not enough seasoning or not enough texture. This recipe solves both. The soy-garlic-ginger marinade saturates the tofu with umami, and the cornstarch coating creates a thin, crispy shell that shatters when you bite into it. The contrast between the crunchy exterior and the soft, creamy interior is what makes this recipe work.

Pressing the tofu is non-negotiable. Tofu is packed with water, and that water prevents both seasoning absorption and crispiness. Wrapping it in paper towels and pressing it under a heavy object for 15 to 30 minutes removes enough moisture to transform the texture completely. If you cook tofu straight from the package without pressing, no amount of cornstarch will save it.

In the Gulf, tofu has found a growing audience among health-conscious eaters and the vegetarian and vegan communities across Dubai, Abu Dhabi, and the wider region. This recipe works as a protein for grain bowls, a stir-fry component, a salad topper, or simply as a snack with dipping sauce.

**Pro Tip:** Use extra-firm tofu. Firm works in a pinch, but silken or soft tofu will crumble and stick to the basket.39:T631,Cauliflower is one of the most underrated vegetables for the air fryer. The circulating heat hits every surface of each floret, crisping the edges to a deep golden brown while keeping the interior tender and slightly nutty. In a conventional oven, this level of caramelisation takes 30 to 40 minutes and usually requires flipping. In the air fryer, you get there in 15 minutes with a single shake of the basket.

The garlic and parmesan here are not just seasoning. They are architecture. The parmesan melts into a thin, crispy crust on the surface of each floret, and the garlic toasts to a sweet, mellow flavor that bears no resemblance to the sharp bite of raw garlic. Together, they transform plain cauliflower into something that genuinely competes with fries and chips for snacking appeal.

The trick to great air fryer cauliflower is not overcrowding the basket. Cauliflower releases moisture as it cooks, and if the florets are packed in too tightly, they steam instead of roasting. The difference between crowded cauliflower (soft, pale, slightly mushy) and properly spaced cauliflower (crispy, golden, caramelised) is dramatic.

In Gulf kitchens, cauliflower has always been popular, used in everything from Indian-influenced gobi dishes to Arabic-style fried cauliflower with tahini. The air fryer version adds a Western twist with parmesan while keeping the simplicity that makes cauliflower a weeknight staple.

**Pro Tip:** Add the parmesan during the last 3 minutes of cooking, not at the start. This prevents it from burning and gives you a golden, crispy cheese crust.3a:T451,Use 135°C (275°F). An air fryer cooks with fast-moving dry air from an element just above the food (see [how an air fryer works](/beyond-the-recipes/how-does-an-air-fryer-work/)), so the shell sits in air well above the 100°C of boiling water. 135°C is low enough that the outside of the white stays tender while the heat works into the yolk, and high enough to finish a hard-boiled egg in about a quarter of an hour.

Going lower is fine. At 120°C (250°F) the eggs take 1 to 2 minutes longer at each stage and the whites are slightly softer. Going higher causes problems. At 150°C (300°F), a common setting because it is the lowest air-fry temperature on some models, hard-boiled eggs are ready in about 13 to 15 minutes, but the whites are firmer and brown spots are more likely where the shell touches the basket. At 180°C (350°F) or above, the shell can crack as the air inside expands, and the white near the shell turns rubbery before the yolk sets.

If your air fryer's air-fry mode will not go below 150°C, check the Bake or Roast modes, which on some machines allow lower temperatures.3b:T418,Chill them properly and use eggs that are a week or two old. Both matter more than how the eggs were cooked.

An ice bath does two jobs. It stops carryover cooking, and as the egg cools, the white contracts slightly and pulls away from the membrane under the shell. Ten minutes in iced water, until the egg feels cold in your hand, is the target for hard-boiled eggs. Peeling them warm is the most common reason shells come away in tiny pieces with chunks of white attached.

Very fresh eggs are harder to peel whatever you do. A fresh egg white has a lower pH and bonds tightly to the membrane. Over a week or so in the fridge, carbon dioxide escapes through the porous shell, the pH of the white rises and the bond weakens. If you buy eggs from a farm or a weekly market, keep a few back for boiling.

Start peeling at the wide end, where the air pocket gives you a gap to get under the membrane, and peel under running water so it can slide between shell and white. If the membrane is stubborn, slip a teaspoon under it and run it round the egg.3c:T419,Raw brats take 12 to 15 minutes at 190°C (375°F), turned once halfway. Thickness decides where you land in that range: a 3cm link is usually done at 13 minutes, a fat 3.5cm butcher's brat closer to 15. Chicken bratwurst cooks in about the same time as beef but must reach 74°C (165°F) rather than 71°C (160°F), so check it with a thermometer rather than going by colour.

At 200°C (400°F) raw brats are ready in 10 to 12 minutes, which is why many recipes use it. On thicker links, though, the casing tightens faster than the filling expands and often splits along one side, and juice runs out through the split. At 190°C that rarely happens.

Leave space between them. Brats that touch stay pale where they meet, because the hot air can't reach that strip of casing.

Oven-style air fryers with racks brown more slowly on the underside, so turn the brats twice instead of once and add 2 to 3 minutes. For more on why spacing and fan speed matter, see [how an air fryer actually cooks food](/beyond-the-recipes/how-does-an-air-fryer-work/).3d:T568,**From frozen:** don't thaw them. Start raw frozen brats at 180°C (350°F) for 10 minutes, so the centre thaws before the casing over-browns, then turn them, raise the heat to 200°C (400°F) and cook for another 8 to 10 minutes. If the links are frozen together, run the pack under cold water for 30 seconds and prise them apart with a butter knife. Always confirm the centre temperature, because frozen brats vary more than fresh ones.

**Precooked or smoked brats** are already safe to eat, so you're only reheating and browning them: 6 to 8 minutes at 190°C (375°F) until they're steaming hot in the middle.

**Simmered first:** if you like a very juicy brat with a softer bite, simmer raw brats for 10 minutes in salted water or chicken stock with a sliced onion, keeping it just below a boil so the casings don't burst. Drain, pat them dry, then air fry at 200°C (400°F) for 4 to 6 minutes to brown the outside. Simmering is optional; the straight air-fryer method cooks them through safely on its own.

If you'd rather skip the peppers, cook the brats over thick [roasted onion wedges](/recipes/air-fryer-onions/) instead, using the same 5-minute head start. Beef brats leave a film of fat in the drawer either way; wipe it out while it's still warm, and see our [air fryer cleaning guide](/beyond-the-recipes/how-to-clean-air-fryer/) for grease that has already baked on.3e:T49d,Chicken parmesan asks for a crunchy crust with wet sauce and melted cheese sitting on top of it. The air fryer gets the crust right because the fan dries and browns the panko from every side at once. Keeping it crunchy under the topping comes down to timing: sauce and cheese go on only after the chicken is fully cooked, and they get 2 to 3 minutes, enough to melt mozzarella before the tomato has time to soak in.

The method is the same three-step dredge used for our [crumbed chicken tenders](/recipes/air-fryer-chicken-tenders/), with two changes. The panko is mixed with grated hard cheese and rubbed with a tablespoon of olive oil before it touches the chicken, so it browns evenly without patchy spraying. And the chicken is pounded to an even 1.5 cm, so the thin edges don't dry out while the thick end catches up.

The marinara is a 15-minute pan sauce of tinned tomatoes, garlic, oregano and olive oil. It contains no alcohol, and it's cooked thicker than most jarred sauces, which matters here. If you'd rather skip the crumb coating, the section below covers a no-breading version built on plain [seasoned thin cutlets](/recipes/air-fryer-thin-sliced-chicken-breast/).3f:T43a,Water is almost always the reason. A canned chickpea is soft, cooked and well over half water by weight. The crunch only comes when enough of that water has left, and it can only leave through the skin. If you oil the chickpeas at the start, the oil seals the surface, the outside browns fast and the inside stays damp. Within an hour that moisture migrates outwards and the crust goes chewy.

Three other things cause soft chickpeas:

- **Too many in the basket.** A single 400g can fills a 5 to 6 litre basket in one layer. Two cans piled up steam each other, so cook them in two batches.
- **Pulling them too early.** Golden brown is not the same as done. Break one open: if the centre is creamy, they need longer.
- **Storing them warm in a sealed jar.** Trapped steam softens everything. Cool them completely on an open plate first.

If you live somewhere humid, as much of the Gulf coast is from June to September, chickpeas pick up moisture from the air faster. Cook the extra-crunchy version at 180°C (350°F) for 22 to 25 minutes if you plan to keep them for a day or two.40:T489,Each blend is for one 400g can. Add it with the oil after the first 10 minutes, never at the start.

| Blend | What goes in |
|---|---|
| Za'atar and sumac | 1½ tsp za'atar, ½ tsp sumac, ½ tsp salt. Add a squeeze of lemon after cooking. |
| Baharat | 1 tsp baharat, ½ tsp salt, pinch of chilli flakes |
| Chaat masala | 1 tsp chaat masala, ¼ tsp chilli powder, salt to taste (chaat masala is already salty) |
| Lime and chilli | ½ tsp chilli powder, ½ tsp salt, finely grated zest of 1 lime after cooking |
| Garlic and parmesan | ½ tsp garlic powder, ½ tsp salt; toss with 2 tbsp finely grated parmesan as soon as they come out (choose a microbial-rennet hard cheese if you need it halal-certified) |
| Cinnamon sugar | Swap the olive oil for melted coconut oil; toss with 1 tbsp sugar and ½ tsp cinnamon after cooking, not before, because sugar burns at 200°C |

Sugar and cheese go on after cooking. Sugar blackens within a couple of minutes at this heat, and grated cheese melts into the basket mesh. For more ideas on what to roast alongside, see our [time and temperature chart for vegetables](/beyond-the-recipes/air-fryer-vegetable-recipes/).41:T440,Time frozen salmon by its thickness, not its weight. Heat moves into a fillet from the top and bottom faces, so a long, thin 170g tail piece cooks far faster than a compact 170g centre cut. Measure the thickest point with a ruler before you start and use the chart above.

The USDA's general guidance is that frozen food takes about 50% longer to cook than thawed, and salmon follows that closely: a 2.5cm fillet that needs 8 to 10 minutes thawed needs 12 to 15 from frozen. The centre has to climb from freezer temperature, around -18°C (0°F), to 63°C (145°F), and most of the extra time goes on thawing the core.

For loins 4cm or thicker, turn the air fryer down to 190°C (375°F). At 200°C the outer layer dries out before heat reaches the middle of a thick piece. If one batch has mixed thicknesses, put the thick fillets in 3 to 4 minutes before the thin ones so they finish together.

A 5 to 6 litre basket holds four standard portions; in a 3 to 4 litre basket, cook two at a time. Oven-style models often run a minute or two slower, so trust the thermometer over the clock.42:T4df,Raw grated potato releases a lot of water and tends to fall apart in hot air. Parboil the potatoes whole first and the partly cooked starch binds the shreds together, which also come out drier.

1. Put 600g whole, unpeeled floury potatoes in a pan of cold salted water. Bring to the boil and simmer for 8 minutes, until a knife goes in 1cm and then meets firm resistance. Do not cook them through.
2. Drain and cool completely, at least 1 hour at room temperature or overnight in the fridge. Cold potato grates into clean shreds; warm potato turns to mash.
3. Rub off the skins with your thumbs. Grate the potatoes on the coarse side of a box grater.
4. Squeeze the grated onion hard in a tea towel, then mix it gently into the potato with the cornflour, salt and pepper. Use your fingertips so the shreds stay separate.
5. Shape into 8 ovals about 1cm thick and 9cm long. Press them firmly so they hold together.
6. Brush both sides with oil. Air fry at 200°C (400°F) for 15 to 18 minutes, flipping at 9 minutes, until deep golden.

To freeze, lay the shaped, unbaked patties on a lined tray, freeze until solid, then stack them between squares of baking paper in a bag. Cook from frozen at 200°C (400°F) for 18 to 22 minutes, flipping at 10.43:T406,Mushrooms are about 90 percent water, and that is what decides how they turn out in an air fryer. In the first few minutes they release a lot of liquid. If the basket is crowded or the mushrooms were salted, that liquid has nowhere to go and they simmer in it, turning grey and rubbery. With space around them and no salt yet, the moving air carries the water off quickly, and only then does the surface start to brown.

This recipe tosses the mushrooms in plain oil, roasts them for 8 minutes, and only then adds butter, fresh garlic and salt for a final 2 to 3 minutes. Fresh garlic needs about that long to soften and turn pale gold. Put it in at the start and it is black and bitter before the mushrooms are done.

Serve them with [garlic butter steak bites](/recipes/air-fryer-steak-bites/), spoon them over toast, or pile them next to a pan of [Gulf shakshuka](/recipes/gulf-shakshuka/) at breakfast. For a fuller side, roast them with [air fryer onions](/recipes/air-fryer-onions/) using the timing in the variations below.44:T49e,The quickest route to good air fryer potatoes is a bag of baby potatoes cut in half. The skins are thin enough to leave on and the waxy flesh turns creamy rather than dry. Each half also has a flat face that browns hard against the hot basket. They take 15 to 18 minutes at 200°C (400°F) with almost no knife work.

If all you have is large potatoes, cut them into even 2.5cm cubes and follow the same method: rinse, dry, oil, season, shake. Timings for every cut are in the chart, from whole mini potatoes to smashed. For thick, fluffy-centred spears for dipping, see our [seasoned air fryer potato wedges](/recipes/air-fryer-potato-wedges/), and for a whole spud with a split top and toppings, our [air fryer baked potato with crispy salted skin](/recipes/air-fryer-baked-potato/).

In Gulf supermarkets, baby potatoes often come in net bags with sizes from marble to almost 5cm. Spend a minute sorting them before you cut: leave the smallest whole, halve the middle sizes and quarter the biggest, so every piece is about the same thickness. For a sweeter side in the same basket, try [diced sweet potato with smoked paprika or cinnamon](/recipes/air-fryer-sweet-potato-cubes/).45:T4f4,Halved baby potatoes take 15 to 18 minutes at 200°C (400°F), and 2.5cm cubes of large potatoes take 18 to 22 minutes. Whole baby potatoes take longer, 20 to 25 minutes, because the skin slows moisture loss and heat has further to travel to the centre. That is also why whole ones come out creamy and soft while halves come out crisp.

Size matters more than variety. Every extra centimetre adds roughly 5 minutes. Baby gold potatoes are slightly drier and brown a touch faster; baby red potatoes are waxier and need a minute or two more before they feel tender.

Load is the other variable. Halves need a single layer. Cubes cope with two layers if you shake hard. Fill the basket to the brim and the top layer shields the bottom one, so you get pale, steamed potatoes underneath even after 30 minutes. For six people, two batches are faster in total than one crammed basket. Oven-style air fryers with trays sit the food further from the element, so add 3 to 5 minutes and swap the trays halfway.

For dinner from one machine, cook the potatoes first and keep them warm on a tray in a low oven, then use the hot basket for [crispy-skinned air fryer chicken thighs](/recipes/air-fryer-chicken-thighs/) or [salmon fillets with crisp skin](/recipes/air-fryer-salmon/).46:T43f,Any of them will cook well. They differ in texture, and in how well they survive being shaken.

- **Baby potatoes (gold, red or purple):** young, waxy and thin-skinned. Creamy inside and crisp on the cut face, with no peeling. The least work and the most reliable batch.
- **Yukon Gold and other all-rounders:** medium starch with a buttery interior. Cubes hold their shape, and the thin skin can stay on.
- **Russet and other floury potatoes (Maris Piper, King Edward):** high starch, low moisture. Cubes roughen and crisp hard on the outside and turn fluffy inside, but the corners can break when shaken. The broken bits go extra crunchy.
- **Large red-skinned and other waxy potatoes:** hold their shape but brown less and stay dense inside when cubed.

Loose potatoes in Gulf supermarkets are often labelled by country of origin rather than variety. To check, cut one in half and rub the faces together. If they stick and leave a white, starchy film, the potato is floury and will crisp well as cubes. If the faces feel wet and slide, it is waxy and better halved or cut into chunks.47:T44e,No. Halved baby potatoes and 2.5cm cubes cook through in the air fryer alone. Rinsing cubes of large potatoes for 30 seconds is worth doing to wash off loose surface starch that otherwise glues them together and scorches. Long soaks matter more for thin fries than for cubes.

Boiling first is worth it in two cases. **Parboiling cubes** for 6 minutes, then steam-drying and shaking them in the colander, roughens the surface into a thicker, glassier crust like a Sunday roast potato and cuts the air fryer time to 15 to 18 minutes. **Smashed potatoes** need boiling, because the potato has to be fully tender before you flatten it.

**How to make smashed baby potatoes:** boil 750g whole baby potatoes in well-salted water for 12 to 15 minutes, until a knife slides in easily. Drain and steam dry for 5 minutes. Press each one with the base of a glass to about 1.5cm thick, so the skin splits and the edges go ragged. Brush with 2 tbsp olive oil, season, and air fry at 200°C (400°F) for 15 to 18 minutes without turning, until the edges are dark golden and crunchy. Lift them out with a fish slice.48:T45a,Sirloin is the best all-round choice: tender, beefy and cheaper than ribeye. Use any steak you would happily grill whole.

- **Sirloin.** Labelled "top sirloin" on American beef, "rump" on British and Australian beef. Lean, with enough flavour to stand up to garlic butter.
- **Striploin (New York strip).** A fat cap down one side; trim it before cubing or it stays chewy.
- **Ribeye.** The juiciest option. The fat marbling melts at 200°C, so it forgives an extra minute.
- **Tenderloin (fillet).** Very tender, but mild and expensive. Cut bigger cubes, 4cm, and keep it rare to medium.

Avoid stewing beef, chuck and pre-cut "beef cubes". They're full of connective tissue that only softens after an hour or more of slow cooking, so a few minutes in the air fryer leaves them tough.

Much of the fresh beef in Gulf supermarkets is imported and halal-certified, and the labels follow the exporting country's naming, so the same sirloin cut can be sold as "rump steak" in one shop and "top sirloin" in the next. If you're unsure, ask the butcher counter for a steak at least 3cm thick so the cubes come out even.49:T421,For 2.5cm cubes at 200°C (400°F), allow 5 to 6 minutes for medium-rare, 6 to 8 minutes for medium and 9 to 11 minutes for well done, shaking once at 4 minutes. Bigger 4cm cubes add about 2 minutes to each stage.

Those times assume a preheated basket and a single layer. A crowded basket cooks the steak in its own steam: the juices pool in the gaps, the surface stays grey and the inside overcooks while you wait for colour. If you see liquid collecting around the cubes after the shake, the batch was too big.

Small cubes carry very little heat over once they leave the basket, usually 1 to 2°C, so pull them at the temperature you want to eat. USDA guidance for whole cuts of beef is 63°C (145°F) followed by a 3-minute rest. Rare and medium-rare are below that and are a personal choice; serve medium or above to pregnant guests, young children and anyone with a weakened immune system.

Without a thermometer, press a cube with tongs. Medium-rare still yields, medium springs back, and well done feels firm. If in doubt, cut the largest one open.4a:T4cb,At 180°C (360°F), turkey bacon takes 5 to 6 minutes for chewy, 7 to 8 minutes for crisp edges and 9 to 10 minutes for strips that snap, turned once at 4 minutes. Brands vary a lot: some halal turkey bacon is thin and pressed, some is cut thick from smoked turkey thigh, and some has more sugar in the cure. Watch the first batch from about 6 minutes and note the time that gives you the texture you like.

The colour change is your best cue. Raw-looking turkey bacon is pink and glossy; it turns brick-red, then deep reddish-brown, and the edges go dry and rippled as the water cooks out. If the edges are blackening while the middle is still soft, the heat is too high or the slices were overlapping.

Most turkey bacon is sold fully cooked and smoked, so you're cooking for texture rather than safety. If your pack doesn't say "fully cooked" or tells you to cook it thoroughly, make sure it reaches 74°C (165°F), which it will long before it's crisp.

**Air fryer ovens:** use the air-fry setting with the strips on the mesh tray in the upper-middle position. There's more room so you can cook a whole pack at once, but the tray nearest the element browns faster, so swap trays at the turn and allow 1 to 2 extra minutes.4b:T45d,Turkey bacon is thin and light, and the fan in a basket air fryer is strong enough to lift a strip off the base, especially in the first minute before it has dried and stiffened. A strip that sticks to the heating element can scorch and smoke.

Three fixes, in order of how well they work:

1. **Weigh it down.** A small wire rack, an air fryer trivet or the rack from a toaster oven laid on top holds the strips flat and still lets air through.
2. **Don't preheat a hot, empty basket.** Load the strips first and then start the machine, or preheat for no more than 2 minutes.
3. **Never put loose parchment in alone.** A paper liner without food on it can blow up into the element. If you use a perforated liner, lay the turkey bacon on it before the basket goes in, so the strips hold it down.

A liner makes clean-up easier, but it also stops air reaching the underside, so lined turkey bacon needs 1 to 2 more minutes and must be turned. Egg white begins to set at around 62°C and turns fully firm near 80°C, while the yolk thickens from about 65°C and sets solid at around 70°C. The basket's mesh or grill pattern conducts heat more intensely than the air, and the white directly underneath can scorch slightly. It is cosmetic, but you can prevent it by standing eggs in silicone muffin cups, on a wire rack, or on a small ring of scrunched foil. Baked-on grease in the basket makes it worse, so a clean basket helps; our guide to [cleaning an air fryer basket](/beyond-the-recipes/how-to-clean-air-fryer/) covers removing that residue.\n\nCracks happen when the temperature is too high or the eggs already have hairline fractures. This page is written for halal beef or chicken bratwurst only.\n\nIn the Gulf, halal beef and chicken sausages, including bratwurst-style links, are usually in the chilled sausage and cold-cuts section of larger supermarkets and hypermarkets, with frozen packs in the freezer aisle nearby. Where non-halal meat is sold at all, it sits in a separate, signposted non-Muslim section, so it's easy to avoid. Some halal butchers also make fresh beef sausages to order if you ask for a coarse grind with nutmeg, white pepper and marjoram, the classic bratwurst seasoning.\n\nOutside the Gulf, look for a recognised halal certification mark on the pack, not just the word \"beef\". Check the casing line in the ingredients: collagen casings made from beef, or sheep casings, are what you want. A pack that says \"natural casing\" with no halal mark could have a non-halal casing even if the meat is beef."}],"tips":["Use a thermometer the first time you cook a new brand. Halal brats range from lean chicken links to fatty beef ones, and fat content changes the timing by a few minutes.","Turn with tongs, not a fork. Sogginess comes from water moving out of the tomato into the crumb. A sauce reduced until it holds a trail has far less free water than a runny jarred sauce, and the bare 1 to 2 cm border around each cutlet stays as crunchy as it was before the topping went on.\n\nCheese choice matters for the same reason. Low-moisture mozzarella (the firm block, not the balls in brine) melts into a stretchy layer without weeping. Fresh mozzarella releases liquid as it heats; if you want to use it, tear it, pat it dry on kitchen paper and add it for no more than 2 minutes.\n\nFinally, rest the cooked cutlets on a rack rather than a flat plate. A whole breast is often 3 cm at one end and 1 cm at the other, so by the time the centre reaches 74°C (165°F) the thin end is dry. The same check applies to mozzarella and to any shop-bought breaded chicken.\n\nFor the topping, grate the mozzarella yourself if you can. It happens most in the first 5 to 8 minutes and it is harmless. Drying the chickpeas properly and removing loose skins cuts it down a lot. Popping chickpeas can jump against the heating element, so check under the crisper plate when you clean up; a small burnt chickpea there will smoke the next time you cook. Cooking fish straight from the freezer is safe as long as the thickest part reaches 63°C (145°F), the USDA safe minimum for fish. In a hot air fryer the fish moves quickly through the 4 to 60°C (40 to 140°F) danger zone, rather than sitting in it for hours the way a fillet left to thaw on the counter does.\n\nTake vacuum-packed fish out of its pack before it starts to thaw; the FDA flags thawing fish in reduced-oxygen packaging as a botulism risk. And do not leave frozen salmon on the worktop to soften. In a Gulf kitchen in summer, the outside of a fillet can sit at room temperature for hours while the middle is still frozen.\n\nA probe thermometer is the most reliable check. Frozen fillets cook less evenly than thawed ones, so the edges can look done while the middle is still cold. Mixing the spices into oil rather than sprinkling them dry also stops the fan blowing loose powder around the basket.\n\nSauces containing sugar, such as honey, a sweet chilli glaze or bottled teriyaki-style sauces, go on even later: brush them on for the final 3 minutes only, or they burn. If you are using a bottled sauce, check the label, because many teriyaki sauces contain alcohol."},{"id":"skin-on-frozen-salmon","heading":"Skin-on or skinless frozen fillets?","body":"Both work, with the same timings. Thin or mini patties are done in 8 to 10 minutes. Thick patties of 1.5cm or more do better at 190°C (375°F) for 13 to 16 minutes, so the centre heats through before the faces go too dark.\n\nThe number of patties barely changes the time, as long as they sit in one layer. What changes it is crowding: overlap two patties and the covered section needs several extra minutes and still won't crisp.\n\nPackage instructions are written for an oven or a frying pan, so treat them as a starting point only. The air fryer usually needs less time than the oven figure. For other frozen breakfast staples, our [frozen food air fryer cooking times chart](/beyond-the-recipes/air-fryer-frozen-food-cooking-times/) covers how to convert oven instructions."},{"id":"flip-thaw-oil","heading":"Do you need to flip, thaw or oil hash brown patties?","body":"**Flip: yes.** The underside sits on the basket and gets less airflow, so it stays paler. One flip at the halfway point gives both faces the same colour. A few air fryers with strong top-and-bottom airflow manage without it, but flipping costs ten seconds and removes the guesswork.\n\n**Thaw: no.** Frozen patties hold their shape and go into a hot basket firm. Thawed ones soften, stick and tear when flipped. If yours have thawed by accident, cook them for 7 to 9 minutes and flip gently.\n\n**Oil: optional.** Frozen patties are par-fried before freezing, so there is already oil in them. A light spray on each side gives a deeper colour and a slightly crunchier surface. Use a refillable pump sprayer or a brush rather than an aerosol can: the propellant in aerosols can leave a sticky residue that builds up on non-stick baskets."},{"id":"crispier-hash-brown-patties","heading":"How to make hash brown patties crispier","body":"Soft patties almost always trace back to one of these:\n\n1. **Preheat the basket.** A cold start means the frost melts slowly and the patty sits in water.\n2. **Leave gaps.** At least 1cm between patties so air can reach the edges.\n3. **Flip at halfway, not earlier.** The first side needs time to set a crust that holds the patty together.\n4. **Add 2 minutes at the end.** If they are cooked but pale, an extra 2 minutes at 200°C (400°F) is usually all they need.\n5. **Rest on a rack.** Steam trapped under a patty on a plate softens the crust fast.\n\nAfter flipping, press each patty lightly with a spatula. More of the surface touches the hot basket and the edges brown further."},{"id":"homemade-hash-brown-patties","heading":"How to make hash brown patties from scratch","body":"$42"},{"id":"halal-hash-brown-patties","heading":"Are frozen hash brown patties halal?","body":"Most frozen hash brown patties contain potato, vegetable oil, salt and a starch or dextrose coating, with no animal ingredients. The cut matters more than the variety. Sliced mushrooms have more exposed surface, so their water leaves quickly and they brown in 7 to 9 minutes. Whole button mushrooms keep their water in for longer and need 10 to 12. Large portobello caps sit better at 190°C (375°F) so the cap cooks through before the rim dries out.\n\nCount the garlic butter stage within those totals. For any cut, add the butter and garlic for the last 2 to 3 minutes.\n\nTwo things stretch the time. The first is a full basket: 450g (1 lb) is the most a 5 to 6 litre basket handles well; above that, cook in two rounds. The second is a small or low-wattage air fryer. White buttons work the same way but end up slightly softer.\n\nPortobellos are the same species left to grow large. Cook them whole as a burger substitute or slice them thickly. They are easy to find in larger supermarkets in Dubai, Abu Dhabi and Riyadh, often in the Asian produce section. Shiitake stems are woody, so remove them and save them for stock."},{"id":"why-soggy","heading":"Why are my air fryer mushrooms soggy?","body":"They steamed. soak potatoes before air frying?","body":"$47"},{"id":"why-not-crispy","heading":"Why are my air fryer potatoes not crispy?","body":"Nearly every soft batch traces back to one of four things:\n\n1. **Wet surfaces.** Water has to evaporate before the surface can get past 100°C and start browning. Dry the potatoes in a towel.\n2. **Overcrowding.** Hot air needs a path around each piece. Single layer for halves, two layers at most for cubes.\n3. **Halves face up.** The cut face browns best against the preheated basket, so put halves cut side down.\n4. **Salting too early.** Salt draws water out. Season just before cooking, not 20 minutes ahead.\n\nWhole baby potatoes are meant to be creamy with blistered skins rather than crunchy; halve them if you want crunch. Potatoes that are crisp at first but soften on the table are steaming in their own heat, so serve them in a wide, shallow dish and don't cover them. White smoke or a burnt smell early in cooking usually means old oil in the drawer, and our [step-by-step air fryer cleaning guide](/beyond-the-recipes/how-to-clean-air-fryer/) covers how to clear it."}],"tips":["Sort a mixed bag by size before you start. Cube size matters more than the total weight in the basket, because heat only has to travel about 1.25cm to reach the centre of each piece. Drop to 2cm cubes and you are done in 5 to 6 minutes. Go up to 4cm and you need closer to 10.\n\nKeep the heat high. Browning (the Maillard reaction) only gets going once the surface is dry and well above 100°C, and at 180°C (350°F) the inside is cooked before the outside has time to colour.\n\nCheck the biggest cube, not the smallest. A probe thermometer pushed into the middle should read 63°C (145°F), the USDA safe temperature for fish. White beads on the surface are albumin, a protein squeezed out as the muscle fibres tighten. A few are normal; a crust of them means the salmon has gone a minute or two too far.\n\nOven-style air fryers with trays run slower, so rotate the tray halfway and expect to add a minute."},{"id":"why-cornflour-and-sauce-after","heading":"Why use cornflour, and why add the sauce after cooking?","body":"Salmon is wet, and wet surfaces steam instead of browning. A teaspoon of cornflour tossed over oiled cubes soaks up that surface moisture and sets into a very thin crust in the hot air. You get crisp corners without a breadcrumb coating, and the sauce has something to cling to afterwards.\n\nThe honey goes in last because sugar scorches. At 200°C a glaze brushed on at the start blackens before the fish is cooked, and whatever drips through the basket smokes on the drip tray. Simmering the sauce in a pan for two minutes thickens it properly, and tossing the cooked bites through it coats every side. If you want stickier, slightly caramelised edges, put the glazed bites back on a parchment-lined basket for 1 minute at 200°C."},{"id":"salmon-bites-sauces","heading":"Honey garlic, spicy and other sauces for salmon bites","body":"Each of these makes enough for 600g of salmon. Simmer the cooked sauces for 1 to 2 minutes; the dips need no cooking.\n\n- **Spicy honey (hot honey style):** 3 tbsp honey, 1½ tbsp sriracha, 1 tsp lemon juice, pinch of salt. Simmer and toss.\n- **Teriyaki-style, alcohol-free:** 2 tbsp halal soy sauce, 2 tbsp honey, 1 tsp grated ginger, 1 grated garlic clove, 3 tbsp water and ½ tsp cornflour stirred into the cold liquid. Simmer until glossy. Traditional teriyaki is made with alcohol-based seasonings; this version gets its shine from the cornflour instead.\n- **Bang bang dip:** 3 tbsp mayonnaise, 1 tbsp sweet chilli sauce, 1 tsp sriracha, squeeze of lime. Serve on the side so the bites stay crisp.\n- **Lemon and sumac yoghurt:** 120g thick yoghurt or labneh, 1 tsp sumac, zest of half a lemon, pinch of salt. A cooling dip for summer, when a sticky glaze feels heavy."},{"id":"choosing-salmon","heading":"Which salmon to buy for salmon bites","body":"Buy a centre-cut fillet about 2.5 to 3cm thick. It gives you even cubes with little waste, whereas the tail end is thin enough to dry out before the thicker pieces are cooked. Skinless saves you a job, and most fish counters will remove the skin if you ask.\n\nFarmed Atlantic salmon, the kind most Gulf supermarkets stock, has more fat between the flakes and stays juicy if you overshoot by a minute. Wild sockeye or coho is leaner and finishes about a minute sooner, so start checking at 5 minutes.\n\nFrozen portions work if you thaw them first. Move them to the fridge the night before, or cut them while still half-frozen, when the flesh is firm and slices cleanly, then let the cubes finish thawing for 20 minutes and pat them dry before seasoning. In a pan, the lean strips curl, stick and cook unevenly where they buckle away from the heat. In the air fryer, hot air reaches both sides of a flat strip at once, so it dries and browns evenly, and there's no oil spitting across the hob.\n\nTurkey bacon has very little fat to render, so it crisps by drying out rather than frying in its own fat. Wet turkey bacon steams for the first few minutes and never fully crisps.","Judge crispness after a minute on a rack, not in the basket. Hot turkey bacon is always softer than it will be.","Cut strips in half for a smaller air fryer. Half-strips sit flatter and are less likely to lift in the fan.","For turkey bacon bits, chop the raw strips into 1cm pieces with scissors and shake the basket twice during cooking.","If your first batch curls at the ends, the slices were too close to the element; use a trivet on top or cook on the lower rack."],"substitutions":["Halal beef rashers: cook at 180°C (360°F) for 6 to 8 minutes; they render more fat than turkey.","Smoked turkey or chicken breast slices: cut into strips and cook for 4 to 6 minutes for crisp, smoky pieces.","Use olive oil spray or a little melted butter instead of neutral oil."],"variations":["Honey-pepper: brush the strips with 1 tsp honey mixed with plenty of black pepper in the last 1 to 2 minutes. Earlier and the honey burns.","Za'atar: sprinkle the oiled strips with a pinch of za'atar before cooking.","Turkey bacon-wrapped dates: stuff pitted Medjool dates with an almond, wrap each in half a strip, secure with a cocktail stick and air fry at 190°C (375°F) for 7 to 9 minutes until the turkey bacon is browned. They work well on an iftar table.","Date syrup and chilli: brush with date syrup (dibs) and a pinch of chilli flakes for the last minute."],"servingSuggestions":["Serve alongside eggs, grilled halloumi, tomatoes and toast for a full breakfast plate.","Add to a turkey bacon, lettuce and tomato sandwich on toasted bread.","Crumble over salads, baked potatoes or creamy pasta.","Pair with crisp hash brown patties for a weekend breakfast."],"storage":"Cool completely, then refrigerate cooked turkey bacon in an airtight container lined with paper towel for up to 4 days. Freeze cooked strips between sheets of baking paper for up to 1 month. Raw opened packs keep for the time printed on the label once opened, usually 3 to 5 days.","reheating":"Reheat at 180°C (360°F) for 1 to 2 minutes from the fridge, or 3 to 4 minutes from frozen. The microwave warms it but leaves it soft.","makeAhead":"Cook a full pack, cool it on a rack and refrigerate. On busy mornings it needs only 1 to 2 minutes in the air fryer to crisp again, which is quicker than cooking from raw.","troubleshooting":["Chewy instead of crisp: not cooked long enough, or the strips overlapped. Cook in a single layer and add 1 to 2 minutes.","Burnt edges, soft middle: the temperature was too high. Stay at 180°C (360°F) and turn halfway.","Strips blowing around: weigh them down with a small rack or trivet, and don't preheat an empty basket for long.","Pale and dry: no oil at all. A light brush of oil on each side helps the surface brown.","Smoke: an old build-up of grease in the drawer is burning. Clean the drawer; turkey bacon itself gives off very little fat."],"faqs":[{"question":"How long do you cook turkey bacon in the air fryer?","answer":"Cook turkey bacon at 180°C (360°F) for 7 to 9 minutes for crisp strips, turning once at 4 minutes, or 10 minutes if you want them to snap. For chewy, flexible turkey bacon, stop at 5 to 6 minutes. Later batches cook about a minute faster because the air fryer is already hot."},{"question":"Does turkey bacon get crispy in the air fryer?","answer":"Yes. The circulating air dries the lean strips evenly, which is how turkey bacon crisps. Lay it in a single layer, brush lightly with oil and cook at 180°C (360°F) for 7 to 9 minutes for crisp edges, or 9 to 10 for strips that snap. Let it cool on a rack for a minute to firm up."},{"question":"Do you need to flip turkey bacon in the air fryer?","answer":"Yes, turn it once halfway through. The underside gets less airflow from the basket, so unturned strips crisp on top and stay soft underneath. Turning is especially important if you use a parchment liner."},{"question":"Can you cook frozen turkey bacon in the air fryer?","answer":"Yes. Separate the frozen slices first; a quick 20 to 30 seconds on the microwave defrost setting helps. Lay them flat and cook at 180°C (360°F) for 8 to 11 minutes, turning at 5 minutes. A frozen block won't cook evenly."},{"question":"Should I put parchment paper under turkey bacon in the air fryer?","answer":"Only if it's weighed down by the turkey bacon, and preferably perforated. Loose parchment can blow into the heating element. A liner blocks airflow underneath, so add 1 to 2 minutes and turn the strips to crisp both sides."},{"question":"Is turkey bacon already cooked?","answer":"Most turkey bacon is sold fully cooked and smoked, so you are heating it and crisping it. Check the pack. If it isn't labelled fully cooked, cook it until it reaches 74°C (165°F), which happens well before it is crisp."},{"question":"Is air-fried turkey bacon healthy?","answer":"Air frying adds almost no fat, since you only need a light brush of oil. Turkey bacon is usually lean but can be very salty, so check the sodium on the label and treat it as a cured meat rather than a lean protein."}],"relatedRecipeSlugs":["air-fryer-boiled-eggs","air-fryer-hash-brown-patties","gulf-shakshuka"]}]}]}]]}],null,"$L4c"]}]
